#2168. Still unfit and still angry | West Ham fans won’t be at all surprised

Leopards, it is said, do not change their spots. Neither, it would seem, do out-of-favour ‘wantaways’ who refuse to play for one manager having fallen out with his predecessors, despite being made a millionaire by the club that employs him, change their mindsets.

Such would seem to be the case with the talented yet angst-ridden package that is Jean Clair Todibo, once of Barcelona, now of West Ham yet loaned out through sheer frustration to Lens back in his native France.

Yet Todibo hasn’t managed to make the mindset leap towards peace, contentment, and a regular starting slot.

It’s all relayed by insidefutbol.com in their article

…Lens boss Yannick Cahuzac has played down West Ham United loanee Jean-Clair Todibo being angry about being substituted off against Lille and insists the defender’s fitness is being worked on.

On Friday, against Lille, the West Ham centre-back started the game and he was substituted in the 62nd minute mark, a decision which left the player visibly frustrated and angry. “

Todibo now on loan at French side Lens but still not pulling up any trees: Is anyone surprised?

Revealing that even now, two and a half years since Todibo’s arrival at West Ham, the player is still not fit for a full game. Surprise, surprise.

Did he ever manage 90 minutes in a West Ham shirt? I think maybe just once. Some players have ‘trouble’ all over them and Todibo, ever since his Barcelona exit, has struggled to find a place where he can hold down a squad place and keep his seemingly volatile nature in check.

Whether he will ever fully solve his ‘fitness issues’ after his dreadful childhood accident, who knows. His very existence near the top of the football pyramid is testament to his enormous mental strength. But more’s the pity that Tim Steidten didn’t choose to give him a very, very wide berth, instead choosing to whisk Todibo to West Ham on the private jet in 2024.

Another one in the ‘West Ham hall of fame crap transfers’: And he’s got another two years on his West Ham contract even after this season’s loan is over.

#2167. ‘Embarrassing themselves!’ Jamie Carragher hits back at Rio Ferdinand over Cristiano Ronaldo defence

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Jamie Carragher has taken aim at Rio Ferdinand for defending Cristiano Ronaldo following the Portuguese forward’s latest controversy with his national team.

Ronaldo has found himself at the centre of a growing dispute after abruptly leaving Portugal’s national team camp following a reported disagreement with head coach Jorge Jesus. The incident has prompted strong reactions from former teammates and pundits, with Ferdinand firmly siding with the Al-Nassr star.

Jamie Carragher, however, believes Ronaldo’s reputation should not shield him from criticism when his behaviour becomes difficult to defend.

Rio Ferdinand, who played alongside Cristiano Ronaldo at Manchester United between 2003 and 2009, criticised Jorge Jesus for the way he handled the situation.

Speaking on his Rio Presents podcast, the former United captain criticised Jesus for not showing respect to Cristiano Ronaldo.

“Cristiano Ronaldo was put in a very bad situation and was treated in an inappropriate way. He has an enormous history in football. Jorge Jesus did not deal with Ronaldo well and chose to treat him in that way, but I cannot find any explanation for it,” Ferdinand said.

Despite Jesus being Portugal’s manager, Ferdinand questioned his standing, comparing him with the likes of Sir Alex Ferguson and Pep Guardiola. To say the least, Ferdinand only embarrassed himself doing that.

Jamie Carragher saw the argument very differently and rightfully so.

Speaking on The Overlap Daily, the former Liverpool defender suggested that Ferdinand’s friendship with Ronaldo was influencing his reaction to the incident.

“People think it’s good to be close to people in the game, but this is where it becomes a problem,” Carragher explained.

He then called out both Ferdinand and broadcaster Piers Morgan, who has also been vocal in his support of Ronaldo.

“People like Piers Morgan and Rio Ferdinand, who are big people in the media whether you like them or not, are embarrassing themselves with the nonsense that they’re coming out with because they know Ronaldo. You can know someone and try to look after them, but you cannot defend the indefensible.”

Carragher believes Ronaldo’s achievements should not put the forward beyond criticism, particularly when his actions involve his national team and its management.

Gary Neville, who also played with Cristiano Ronaldo, has questioned Ronaldo’s handling of the situation, despite his longstanding affection for his former Manchester United teammate.

Neville argued that Ronaldo’s public response had made matters worse and the forward needed to accept criticism rather than expect others to defend him.

“With Cristiano, it’s difficult because I love him, but I can’t watch what happened at United in his second period and then see him make a statement like that without saying something. He has to be pulled up on that and told that it isn’t acceptable,” Neville said.

He also suggested Ronaldo’s extraordinary success, wealth and influence could have contributed to a sense that he was beyond challenge.

“When you have a billion pounds in the bank, the achievements he has had and the power he has, you feel you’re unbreakable, you feel you’re unstoppable,” Neville added.

Despite his criticism, Neville does not believe the episode will define Ronaldo’s legacy permanently. He expects the attention to fade once the forward retires, allowing his achievements on the pitch to take centre stage again.

#2166. Sheffield Wednesday decision Dave Challinor made in midweek has split opinion

Dave Challinor fielded an incredibly young, inexperienced side with numerous debuts handed out as their EFL Trophy group phase ended in heavy defeat against League Two side Accrington Stanley, and the decision to do so has split fan opinion.

Wednesday headed into Wednesday's clash already through in the Northern Group A of the Jersey Mike's Trophy, owing to wins over Sunderland's Under-21s and Salford City over the last international break.

Therefore, new Owls boss Challinor took Wednesday's clash with Accrington as an opportunity to rest some senior players, as various members of the squad battle injury, and hand some academy members an evening they'll never forget, making their debuts at Hillsborough.

The oldest outfield player was 21-year-old Gabriel Otegbayo, and an Accy side that had a mix of teenagers and 39-year-old Shaun Whalley leading the line took advantage to win 4-0 and steal the top spot in the group.

The defeat means that Dave Challinor opens his managerial account at Hillsborough with two defeats in different competitions to League Two opposition, but unlike at Oldham Athletic last Sunday in the FA Cup, this loss against Accrington could have a small asterisk beside it.

Reflecting on the game after the final whistle, Challinor put the onus on a proud night for the academy, with Louis Poland making his senior debut from the off, and Charlie Liddell, Kleven De Oliveira, James Kay, Kailen Hatfield and Fortune Onoh adding to that off the bench.

Barnsley 0-1 Sheffield Wednesday (after extra time), Wembley, 29 May 2023. Type the surnames of Darren Moore's starting XI. Every player you get drops onto the pitch.

You have 90 seconds. Surnames are enough.

Josh Windass headed in Lee Gregory's cross in the 123rd minute to send the Owls up.

"If there's anything to take from it, it's that [a debut at Hillsborough]," he said.

"So whether it's families here, parents, all those sorts of things, I'm sure it will be a night they'll all remember, perhaps not the result they'd want to remember, but the opportunity to play for this club and in this stadium will live with them for the rest of their lives."

It may not have been the ideal result, but the young Wednesday group battled well at 0-0 for 38 minutes, and two late goals from substitutes Charlie Warren and Charlie Caton added some extra gloss to the scoreline for the League Two side.

The majority of the Wednesday fanbase appreciate the decision made by their new manager to hand out debuts and give some senior players a much-needed rest ahead of a return to league action against Plymouth Argyle on Saturday.

"Nothing to lose from tonight. Both clubs are still through to the first round of the sandwich trophy. An ideal time to put youngsters out and see how they fare. As it was a nothing game. Nothing to prove tonight, at all," one fan stated on X.

Another fan commented, "We had already qualified why would he risk any of our first team. Give the kids a run out, move on 🤷🏽‍♂️."

However, there are still some of the more demanding sections of the fanbase who expect Sheffield Wednesday to be competitive in every game they play, and for those who attended the game, they didn't feel as if they got their money's worth.

"Ahhh well, so long as the parents had a good time that's alright," one fan commented in a sarcastic tone, while another added, "Great for the youngsters and their families, but what about the Mugs like me who drove an hour in each direction and paid good money to watch a competitive football match!"

"I know he’s brand new to the post and the game didn’t matter. Losing 4-0 at home to Accrington Stanley is bad in whatever competition #swfc," was also amongst the discourse after the game.

What do you make of Dave Challinor's squad selection on Wednesday night? Let us know in the comments.

However valid you feel both sides of the coin are, it can be agreed that the exposure of some of Wednesday's next generation is, ultimately, a good thing for the club, and Challinor shouldn't, and won't be, judged by this defeat in a competition they'd already qualified out of the group in.

Ultimately, it was a wise decision from Challinor not to risk an already injury-plagued senior squad in a rather meaningless matchup with Accrington Stanley on Wednesday night, especially with the Owls in league action this weekend.

Despite having more than three players out on international duty, not all of them qualify under the EFL quota to warrant a postponement, so Sheffield Wednesday will take on Plymouth in a massive matchup at Hillsborough on Saturday.

FLW gets AI to rank the friendliest clubs in the second tier this season

While Wednesday fans may not have minded an FA Cup run or a more competitve matchup in Challinor's first home game in charge, this is ultimately going to be the game where reactionary early verdicts are given over whether this Owls side will be skyrocketing up the division under their new boss.

Argyle sit three points ahead of Wednesday in the play-offs, so a statement win over Tom Cleverley's side could see the Owls jump back into the top six in Challinor's first game in charge.

It'll be a more energised senior side, given their lack of involvement in midweek, infront of a packed Hillsborough expecting an end to their six-game winless run.

#2165. Success story means West Ham flop could stay away for ever – Report

If ever there was a story detailing the importance of attitude over ability, then this tale of overcoming adversity – in the end –  involving one of West Ham’s worst transfers of the Steidten era – is certainly that.

Having vowed never to write another Niclas Fullkrug story I find myself drawn yet again to his remarkable mental resilience and fortitude, re-launching his career at 34 years old ‘back home’ in Germany after failing so conclusively at both West Ham and AC Milan – just in case anyone wants to point the finger at a Hammers’ coaching failure being solely to blame for his misfiring.

This is a player who lost his international squad spot, couldn’t stay fit for love nor money (although he had plenty of that) and eventually after a very public, failed loan in Italy, rediscovered himself and his form back in his Bundesliga home.

Life in the old dog yet at 34 – Niclas Fullkrug

Fullkrug is now the German top league’s top scorer. Just reflect on that for a second.

The guy who scored three times in two years as a West Ham player. Probably he’d have done better without missing half the club’s games through injury. As Bild.de suggest, Fullkrug has found his happy place and may never leave:

“It is possible!:Will Füllkrug stay with Werder forever?”

Runs their headline, confirming that they want to keep ‘Fulle’ even once his playing days are over:

“There have already been talks about a follow-up contract with us and the idea is still concrete. It is quite conceivable that we will tie him to the club after his career. I really appreciate his football expertise.”

Which suggests that Fullkrug has already done enough to earn another deal at Werder Bremen, meaning he’s likely never, ever coming back. Sadly, neither is the £27 million, or the nine million salary which he’s had as a West Ham player – thats three million per goal. Nice work if you can get it.

Perhaps Niclas Fullkrug responds better to German phrases rather than Anglo/Hispanic instructions. Adding a bit of Italian surely did not make a helpful contribution to his playing style.

Maybe Scott Parker and his English speaking backroom team can integrate an international grade goalscorer into our front-foot pressing side. Maybe more likely that Scott will not have that opportunity.

We have to demand a fee for him at the end of his fantastic goal scoring year (more goals than Kane currently) or tell him to come back. Someone will cough up say 3m for him.

#2164. Is Mainoo-Tielemans the right midfield option for Man Utd?

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Manchester United drew 1-1 at home to Tottenham despite having an extra man advantage. The Reds saw Mbeumo put them in front, but Solanke scored a late leveller in injury time after Bentancur was shown a red card for headbutting Luke Shaw.

Manchester United’s worrisome season continues after accumulating just six points off the first six games, their first start in 40 years. But more than the results, what worries Manchester United is their midfield pairing.

United spent massively on their midfield rebuild and somehow can’t find the best combination in the midfield. Michael Carrick has gone with the Mainoo-Tielemans partnership, but they have failed to control the midfield. Individually, they both come with decent numbers, but collectively they fall short.

Mainoo and Tielemans struggled against Tottenham while closing the midfield and at times showed a lack of energy. On the day when nothing clicked for United, the Mainoo-Tielemans partnership particularly raised eyebrows.

Gary Neville has also raised serious concerns about Manchester United’s Mainoo-Tielemans partnership.

Neville was particularly unimpressed by the midfield balance, arguing that the problems went beyond one disappointing performance.

The former United captain stopped short of ruling out the combination altogether, but he suggested he has serious doubts about its suitability.

“Tielemans and Mainoo, I’m not calling time on it, that’s probably too drastic, but I’ve seen enough of it to think that’s not a midfield that’s dynamic enough to play at this football club in the Premier League.”

Former Spurs midfielder Redknapp felt United struggled to connect their midfield with the rest of the team during the opening stages.

He added: “I didn’t see enough connections in that midfield in the first hour. Baleba came on and did OK and showed a bit more.

“They look devoid of any confidence, any idea, any patterns of play, playing out from the back. Tottenham were much better than them in every single department.”

While Carlos Baleba was a particular highlight on the day, he showed great potential when brought on. He brought a new sense of energy in the midfield and sent a few balls over the top for a couple of quick counterattacks as well.

Redknapp also pointed to the physical demands of modern Premier League football, arguing that United cannot afford to be outworked by their opponents.

Tottenham ran a staggering 5 km more than Manchester United, something Redknapp felt was a pattern.

"Spurs ran 5km more than Man United… it's like that EVERY GAME!"Jamie Redknapp is less than impressed with the lack of ground covered this season by Manchester United 😬 pic.twitter.com/BxWkFuyGJ1

“There is a pattern that I don’t really like when I watch teams, and that’s when every time the stats don’t lie and the other team runs more than you.

“I feel in Premier League football right now there are a couple of non-negotiables, and one is you have to run more than the other team if you want to win or at least get close enough. Tonight Spurs ran 5km more than Manchester United.”

He believes Tottenham’s pressing exposed the lack of pace in United’s midfield, making it difficult for the hosts to move the ball safely through the centre.

“In terms of how they played today, I think they have an issue with Tielemans and Mainoo in midfield. I don’t see enough energy in there. Baleba will add a lot and will give them a tremendous amount of energy but right now, when things aren’t going well… and Tottenham knew it!

“Every time United played the ball out from the back they pounced. They pressed them because they knew United don’t have the pace and speed in midfield to get away.”

“In isolation, one of them is absolutely fine, the two of them together doesn’t work for me. They haven’t got the pace or the energy to get around the pitch for 90 minutes.”

#2163. One lesson Senne Lammens learned after Tottenham’s late equaliser

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Senne Lammens believes Manchester United must learn to finish games off after their late collapse against Tottenham Hotspur.

The result left the goalkeeper frustrated at Old Trafford despite leading the game and with a player more on the field.

Manchester United looked set for three points after Bryan Mbeumo put them ahead in the second half, but Dominic Solanke’s stoppage-time equaliser denied Michael Carrick’s side a much-needed victory.

Speaking to MUTV, Senne Lammens admitted the team had struggled early on but had done enough after the break to put themselves in a winning position.

“Football can be a bit cruel at times,” the Belgian goalkeeper said. “There are a lot of ups and downs, especially in a game like that.”

Senne Lammens felt United failed to take advantage of Tottenham’s red card, allowing the visitors too much space to find a late equaliser.

“We’ve won a lot of games against 10 men [in the past], but you also know there can be a tricky situation when you’re playing against 10 men, because they have nothing to lose and they know that.”

He added that United should have scored again to put the result beyond doubt or, at the very least, prevented Tottenham from creating further chances.

“I think that’s what we did wrong. We gave them a little bit too much breathing room to create those chances, and then one ball has to go right for them, one bounce has to go their way, and they can score like that.”

The goalkeeper also pointed to similarities with United’s earlier disappointment against Everton, where points slipped away late in the match.

“You feel like those games just slip away from you, and there are one or two situations where you don’t really have control,” he said.

“I think there’s a lot to learn from today. It wasn’t all negative, not at all, but especially the way it ended left a very bad feeling.”

“I think the easy answer is that we have to be able to kill the game faster and not put ourselves in that position to give them a chance.”

Despite the frustration, Lammens said there were positives to take from the performance. However, the late goal had left a bitter feeling, and United now need to turn those lessons into better results.

#2162. Max Lowe breaks silence as Sheffield Wednesday situation changes

Dave Challinor has taken over from Henrik Pedersen as the manager of Sheffield Wednesday, and he may find it a bigger challenge than he first thought.

The Owls were defeated by Oldham Athletic of League Two in Round One of the FA Cup last weekend despite lining up with a strong team, before a more youthful side were hammered 4-0 by Accrington Stanley in the EFL Trophy.

Challinor is still yet to take in a league game, which comes this weekend against Plymouth Argyle on home soil, and he'll soon welcome back his former Stockport County favourite Louie Barry into his plans, having been sidelined since early September with an ankle injury.

But with Barry heading back to fitness, Challinor is now having to deal with one more injury to a key player at the opposite end of the pitch.

One of Sheffield Wednesday's key players for a number of years now has been Max Lowe - the left-sided defender has been a top performer and has stuck with the club through their tough period when he could've easily left for another side.

EFL expert Don Goodman has revealed the three clubs across all three tiers to have surprised him the most so far in 2026/27 to Football League World

If there's one drawback to Lowe though, it's his injury record. The defender missed the final eight games of his debut 2024-25 season with a knee injury, and in 2025-26 he'd miss a full three months with a calf problem.

More setbacks followed in late August this year when Lowe suffered with a hamstring injury, and after returning to action a month ago, it's now been revealed that the 29-year-old has yet again suffered a reoccurrence of the problem he picked up two-and-a-half months ago.

Lowe entered the Oldham defeat from the bench after 71 minutes at the weekend, and whilst finishing the entirety of extra time at Boundary Park, the after-effects of his injury means that he's going to be missing for at least four weeks, and potentially up to six.

The Owls and Challinor himself could do with Lowe back for the busy Christmas period, where they'll play three times between Boxing Day and New Year's Day, but given it's his second spell on the sidelines already this season, it's likely that no risks will be taken with the ex-Sheffield United left-back.

And Lowe himself has taken to his Instagram account to send a message to Owls fans regarding his new injury, and it's one that's come from the heart.

Some Sheffield Wednesday fans have been left frustrated at Lowe's newest injury though, with one claiming that he should've been replaced in the summer knowing what his fitness record was like.

And another Owls supporter believes that the medical department itself may need a complete overhaul, given the amount of niggling injuries that keep flaring up across the squad.

If Wednesday were playing in a back four under Challinor, then Reece James would be the obvious replacement like-for-like with Lowe, and that would be the case too in what looks to be the manager's preferred left of a back three.

However, James is also on the sidelines right now, having suffered a groin injury, and he's also expected back around the same time as Lowe.

It'll now likely be Sil Swinkels who comes in for Lowe on the left-hand side of a back three, having served his one-match suspension for a red card against Blackpool, although he too has already had a period on the treatment table.

Challinor may of course look to play a back four in some matches, given his last few months at Stockport saw him pivot to more of a 4-3-3 and sometimes a 4-4-2 given the attacking options he had to choose from, but for now, it may make more sense to utilise the 3-4-2-1, with Jordi Liongola and Yan Valery operating as wing-backs.

#2161. Preston North End fixture offers big opportunity for Scott Parker – here’s why

One way or another, Scott Parker is going to be forced to make changes to his starting eleven against Preston North End when the International break concludes in a little under two weeks’ time.

By the time the Irons get on the team bus, Parker will have had two weeks’ intensive training with his players and will be in a far better position to judge where to switch players and how to switch tactics to start to get a tune out of his Hammers squad.

One player will be sitting out the tie with an enforced suspension after picking up his fifth yellow card this season. It actually gives Parker a big opportunity to try out an alternative pairing at the heart of his defence, without knocking the confidence of his gentle giant Maximilian Kilman who will miss the game.

Airidas Golambeckis has already impressed: perhaps Kilman’s suspension is his moment..

Whether Parker picks Brazilian centre back by trade Morato or teen Hammer Airidas Golambeckis, the Irons’ manager cannot go wrong. If his pairing produces a shut-out then he’d be justified in retaining his inspired ‘pick’ and leaving Kilman on the bench.

If whoever partners Mavropanos doesn’t cover themselves in glory then he can return to Kilman for the following fixture against Portsmouth just three days later.

Either way, it gives fans like me a respite from the endless starting of a tried, tested, and found wanting duo appearing yet again on the team sheet.

There is , too, the hope that an alternative for Kilman might be lurking within the squad, ready and waiting for just such an opportunity. It seems that Ezra Mayers found his way into Parker’s thinking through being in the ‘right place at the right time’: Perhaps Morato or Golambeckis can make the jersey their own? We can but hope.

#2160. Manchester United defence: Why Carrick must trust Yoro and Heaven

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Manchester United endured another embarrassing draw against 10-man Tottenham after failing to register their second win despite bringing in a plethora of players.

Manchester United invested heavily last season in their attacking options, bringing in Bryan Mbeumo and Matheus Cunha.

This season, they overhauled their midfield. United spent massively on Carlos Baleba, Youri Tielemans and Andrey Santos. Despite spending massively, they have failed to produce the control they would have expected in the midfield. The lack of control was evident in the recent Tottenham game, where Mainoo-Tielemans partnership failed to control the game collectively.

The midfield question was covered in our recent report as well, highlighting a lack of control and carried comments from Gary Neville and Jamie Redknapp.

The bigger question is when United will strengthen their defence. As per our previous report as well, Manchester United are planning to overhaul their defence in the upcoming transfer windows.

United were heavily linked with several left backs but eventually failed to bring any one. The situation left Luke Shaw as the only senior option in the position, while Patrick Dorgu is used upfront by Michael Carrick.

Harry Amass is another option for Michael Carrick, but he is only here because of a failed transfer move. He has been rarely used at left back as Dalot and Mazraoui get the nod in the position ahead of him.

The situation at right-back isn’t all good either. Diogo Dalot is the first option there, but he rarely provides the assurance that puts Michael Carrick at ease. Noussair Mazraoui is the second choice at right-back, but his attacking numbers aren’t great either.

Harry Maguire and Lisandro Martinez form up United’s centre-back pairing while Leny Yoro and Ayden Heaven are used all over the defensive line in rotation when needed. Yoro was used at right-back against Tottenham while Heaven arrived as left-back towards the end of the game.

Manchester United’s season hasn’t been defensively strong or anywhere near being good. They have conceded goals in all Premier League games and only kept one clean sheet against Sabah in the Champions League.

Lisandro Martinez has come under scrutiny after errors on four occasions. Against Ipswich Town, Manchester United won 5-2, but his error resulted in Ipswich’s second goal.

His own goal against Fulham gave away two points at Craven Cottage in the 1-1 draw. Another error led to Solanke’s goal against Tottenham, when he took his eyes off the ball.

His injury concerns are another aspect, and maybe Michael Carrick needs to make a decision.

Manchester United’s success depended heavily on youth over the years. When pundits questioned them, Sir Alex Ferguson gave them the chance to prove them wrong. Alan Hansen made the famous quote “You can’t win anything with kids” on the BBC’s Match of the Day on August 19, 1995. United did exactly that, played youth and went on to win everything.

Michael Carrick needs to do exactly that. He needs to trust youth and allow them to make mistakes. Leny Yoro and Ayden Heaven arrived as massive potentials, but they haven’t been given the license to express themselves, without fearing for their places. Both the youngsters have rarely started together or in a stretch of games. It is maybe time for them to take their chances.

Michael Carrick needs to keep Harry Maguire as a senior experienced figure around them. There is no doubt over Martinez’s ability, but he is not the answer to United’s long-term problem. United need to blend youth with experience as their identity moving forward. Lisandro Martinez perhaps isn’t the answer to United’s problem.

A defensive revival expected in the upcoming transfer windows needs to happen now. It must happen with chances to the Youth. Michael Carrick needs to be brave enough and throw the youngsters in the mix. What it will bring is a long-term solution. They need to experience the errors and pressures that will shape them in football.

#2157. Jamie Carragher & Gary Neville discuss one thing they 'don't like' in the EFL

EFL supporters will tend to disagree on most topics, but amid the incessant discourse and fierce daily debates, there is one thing that the majority of fans sing from the same hymn sheet with.

Club and player performance, divisional standards, club size, fanbase volume and stature and hit-or-miss signings are all subjects which invariably promise to polarise and split opinion, yet that's not the case for every single EFL-related matter.

As such, one notable topic may often spark discussions among supporters of League One and League Two clubs but they rarely disagree on it, and their school of thought also has backing from two of the nation's most prominent pundits in Jamie Carragher and Gary Neville.

That subject is, of course, the EFL trophy, which currently goes under the name of the 'Jersey Mike's Trophy' and is contested by clubs in League One and League Two alongside 16 Category One Premier League under-21 sides.

The formatting of the competition includes one academy team for each group, of which there are a total of 16 on a regionalised basis with eight northern and eight southern, and the top two progress to the knockout stages. All fixtures are regionalised until the semi-final stages, with the final two sides ultimately battling it out for silverware at Wembley.

For a number of reasons, however, the EFL trophy and the requirement of each EFL club outside the Championship to compete in the tournament has long represented a point of often-frustrated discussion, and exactly how Carragher and Neville feel about it has become clearer.

Speaking on the latest episode of The Overlap's Stick to Football podcast, the high-profile punditry pair began the show by discussing the participation of Carragher's son James in the competition for Wigan Athletic's 2-1 Group B victory at Crewe Alexandra on Tuesday evening.

Carragher revealed that he switched the match off at half-time, before Neville disclosed that he doesn't like the competition through his boardroom role with League Two leaders Salford City - who lifted the trophy over Portsmouth behind-closed-doors at Wembley back in 2021.

The ex-Liverpool defender explained: "He (James) played last night. It was on at 7 o'clock, you can get it on Sky and he told me he was playing 45 minutes.

"I just switched it off at half time, so I missed the second half. They won 2-1, I think they had already qualified anyway.

Neville then added: "I don't like that trophy. It's a weird one."

Carragher responded that "it's probably a bit like the Carabao Cup for Premier League teams, but when you get to see something at Wembley," to which Neville recounted: "We (Salford) won it in COVID in League Two, we beat Portsmouth at Wembley.

"We were playing in the final against Portsmouth, it was going to be in March and, when lockdown came in February, they'd sold 32,000 tickets for the final and we were going to make quite a lot of money out of it because we'd sold five or six (thousand).

"It was looking at about 40,000, which is a decent crowd at Wembley and the money splits halfway. We ended up playing it the year after infront of no people, the directors and owners were in there but there was literally nobody in the ground and we had to sit two seats apart from each other.

Football League World assesses and ranks the FIFTEEN best away fanbases in the EFL right now

"We absolutely battered them, it was mad."

The EFL Trophy presents a variety of drawbacks for contestants as EFL outfits face much more jeopardy and risk than the competing under-21 sides, which is a key reason as to why many have slammed the tournament over the years.

Understandably, it is widely felt that the now-11-year-old decision to include Category One academy contingents undermines and devalues the competition's integrity and has created a tournament which is essentially designed to accommodate Premier League clubs, despite the fact that no under-21 side has ever lifted the trophy at Wembley.

Indeed, many age-group contestants will actually take prestigious youth tournaments such as the UEFA Youth League and Premier League International Cup more seriously, which only reduces the competitiveness of the EFL Trophy.

Therefore, the matchday experience for EFL Trophy encounters pales in comparison to any league fixture, as matches are often marked by extremely low attendances and non-existent atmospheres in near-empty stadiums in an accurate display of precisely how most EFL clubs and fanbases alike value the competition.

Another prominent issue is how it provides additional fixture congestion in already-hectic schedules, which proves particularly challenging for sides with weaker or lighter squads.

Naturally, it's frequently to the frustration of promotion-chasing or relegation-battling League One and League Two sides when they have to hop out of league action to compete for the trophy when much greater outcomes and jeopardy are on the line.

Be honest, how do you feel about the EFL Trophy? Do you think that all League One and League Two clubs should have to compete? What would you change about it? Let us know your thoughts in the comments below!

EFL rules mandate the fielding of a minimum number of experienced players, and some clubs can feel the benefits of promoting young talent into senior football through the competition, but the injury risks and fixture congestion often outweigh the advantages and you would be hard-pressed to find too many third and fourth-tier outfits who genuinely value their own participation.

The disadvantages of the EFL Trophy certainly outstrip the benefits for the overwhelming majority of EFL participants, though it's not all universally negative.

Quite obviously, the major upside is the prospect of lifting a trophy at Wembley. We're hardly talking about prestigious silverware here, of course, but either way, that opportunity is one that is nigh-on impossible to experience in any other fashion for almost any non-Championship EFL side unless they win the play-offs, though that's not a classic-style tournament.

Just how much of a benefit that really is remains open to debate, as with only one team capable of winning it per season by definition, everybody else misses out and fails to obtain such an exclusive advantage.

Few would say that trying to get to Wembley in a second-rate competition is of superior importance than trying to battle for promotion or against relegation, too, yet it's a rare upside that can provide supporters with something of a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity.

As aforementioned, meanwhile, the ability to integrate young academy-honed prospects who may not yet be reared and readied for consistent league action into a senior footballing competition, albeit one with debatable pedigree and competitiveness, is still not to be sniffed at.

It can have positive long-term effects, even if the short-term risks mean most clubs would perhaps wish to simply not participate in the first place.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

#2155. Why must Manchester United move beyond the Glazers?

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

The Glazers took control of Manchester United in 2005. That takeover has since caused so many disruptions and protests by the fans over massive debts and insufficient backing of the team in the transfer market, despite successful periods in the Premier League and Europe.

Now Manchester United need to think about who should control the club in the future. Laurie Whitwell has now shared his views on why Manchester United need an ownership change. He simply wants the Glazers gone from Old Trafford. However, he also recognizes that replacing the current ownership doesn’t automatically mean things will improve for United.

The journalist believes the change in ownership has had a lasting effect on United, with the club still feeling its consequences today. A fresh start might be welcome, but the identity of the next owners would matter just as much as the departure of the existing ones.

“Personally, I would like to see the Glazers gone from Manchester United,” Laurie Whitwell said on The Rest is Football podcast with Gary Lineker.

“That change, you know, just really affected and still impacts United now.”

The desire for different ownership is one thing, but finding people capable of taking the club forward is another.

Whitwell admitted there is no perfect answer when it comes to who should take charge.

That leaves United facing a difficult choice. A sale could offer the chance to change how the club is run, but simply bringing in a new owner would not guarantee better decisions or a brighter future.

Whitwell also raised the idea of United supporters having a greater say in the club’s ownership and decisions, much like the Barcelona model where fans own the club itself. However, he highlighted that the Barcelona model isn’t ideal either and has its own shortcomings.

Changing the names at the top would only be one part of the change. The club would also need an ownership structure capable of making sound decisions and providing a direction.

The issue of ownership goes beyond whether the Glazers should stay or leave. Manchester United needs to find the right people and the right model to secure the club’s future. Getting that decision wrong could leave them facing the same circus the fans are enduring under the Glazers.

#2152. 'I've seen him crying' - Sam Clucas reveals Nathan Jones truth at Stoke City

Charlton Athletic boss Nathan Jones has never been one to hide his true emotions, with the Welsh boss wearing his heart on his sleeve, as former Stoke City midfielder Sam Clucas has revealed exactly what he is like behind the scenes.

Nathan Jones has cultivated an excellent coaching career for himself, working his way up the ranks at Yeovil Town, Charlton, and Brighton & Hove Albion before taking the top job at Luton Town, guiding the Hatters from League Two to League One.

He'd leave Kenilworth Road for Stoke City in January 2019, with Luton sat 2nd in the third-tier table at the time and the Potters struggling in the Championship following relegation from the Premier League.

His time at the bet365 Stadium went far from planned, but not for lack of trying, as revealed by former midfielder Sam Clucas.

Football League World has asked AI to rank the top 25 most demanding fanbases in British football right now

Clucas, 36, is now without a club as he enters the twilight of his playing career, but having worked his way up from the National League with Hereford United to Hull City in the Premier League, he joined Swansea City in the summer of 2017 for £16.5 million.

His time in South Wales didn't go as he had hoped, and 12 months later, he joined Stoke in the Championship for £6 million, initially under former Derby County boss Gary Rowett. Six months later, Jones was appointed as the Potters' new head coach, and Clucas has revealed exactly what he was like behind the scenes.

Speaking to The Football Lowdown podcast, Clucas said: "I loved him, to be honest with you.

"I think you'll see from a lot of his interviews that he's definitely a heart-on-his-sleeve type of guy, and believe me, that's not just for show.

"I've seen him crying in dressing rooms after games and stuff; a really emotional guy, but he puts his heart and soul into everything every day in training, his meetings, everything.

"I wanted it to work out so badly for him, to be honest with you, at Stoke, because you could see how much work he was putting in, and I believe he actually slept over at the training ground a few times."

Having succeeded Rowett at the helm in the Potteries, Jones would guide Stoke to 16th in the Championship that year, and over the summer the likes of Nick Powell, Jordan Cousins, Adam Davies, and Liam Lindsay joined under his watch.

However, the Welshman failed to get a tune out of his side, winning two of his first 14 league games in charge the following campaign and was swiftly dismissed.

With a win percentage of just 15.79 per cent, only Paul Lambert and Chris Kamara have a worse win percentage than Jones for Stoke, and of managers to have undertaken 20 games or more for the Potters, he is statistically the worst of all time.

Whenever he and Stoke meet, there is no love between the Welshman and the Potters' supporters, as despite his best efforts, he ultimately flopped in North Staffordshire.

Since leaving Stoke, Jones has returned to Luton, and followed that up with a brief spell with Southampton and now Charlton, with whom he has helped to establish in the Championship after winning promotion with the Addicks back in 2025.

Jones has tended to thrive as an underdog during his coaching career, and taking the Stoke job with the Potters just having been relegated from the Premier League, it was a disastrous fit for all parties.

The Welshman is certainly not the most popular of head coaches, though his heart-on-the-sleeve personality certainly makes for some box office moments, as he looks to steer Charlton up the second tier table this season.

#2149. Rangers transfer hurdle emerges for both West Ham & Wolves with Ryan Naderi

West Ham United and Wolverhampton Wanderers are looking to bolster their forward options this January, as the Championship promotion hopefuls look north of the border for a Czech goal machine.

West Ham and Wolves are preparing for the January window, and have both seemingly earmarked their centre-forward areas as one that requires some improvement, with Rangers striker Ryan Naderi entering the Championship duo's radar.

Naderi has been in excellent form for the Glasgow-based outfit this season, and could be set for a big money move to England this winter, though West Ham and Wolves seemingly face an uphill battle to secure his services with Rangers not willing to sell.

Football League World has asked AI to rank the top 25 most demanding fanbases in British football right now

According to TEAMtalk, both West Ham and Wolves have registered an interest in Czech striker Naderi, with the 23-year-old enjoying an excellent campaign under Derek McInnes at Rangers.

Having spent time coming through the ranks at Dynamo Dresden and Borussia Mönchengladbach in Germany, Naderi propelled himself into the spotlight with Hansa Rostock, before eventually earning a move to Rangers just under 12 months ago for an initial fee of £4.3 million, which could eventually rise to £5.2 million.

In 17 appearances across all competitions this season, Naderi has scored nine and assisted six, battling with Kevin Kelsy, Lawrence Shankland, and Bojan Miovksi for a starting berth at Ibrox with Youssef Chermiti out with a long-term injury.

TEAMtalk reports, however, that Rangers have no intention of allowing Naderi to go anywhere anytime soon, as West Ham and Wolves will have to pay a sizeable chunk to lure him south of the border this winter - a clear stumbling block for both.

West Ham can boast each of Joel Piroe, Taty Castellanos, and Pablo Felipe as their centre-forward options right now, but despite their obvious quality, recently appointed head coach Scott Parker doesn't appear to be convinced by any of them.

The former Burnley boss will be looking to stamp his authority on the Hammers squad this January, and a move for the six-foot-four-inch Naderi poses an attractive option for Parker, who must expect to face strong stubbornness from Rangers in their pursuit.

Wolves, meanwhile, have each of Raul Jimenez, Adam Armstrong, and Sean Neave as options up top, though with Jimenez injured and not getting any younger, Armstrong struggling, and Neave not yet fully trusted, Naderi has been outlined as a candidate that the Black Country outfit could target.

Naderi's natural physicality and goalscoring ability have surely put him on the radar of numerous clubs across Europe, as well as West Ham and Wolves, who must be prepared to splash the cash if they are to lure him to England in the next couple of months.

Scott Parker didn't get the dream start he'd hoped for at West Ham after replacing Nuno Espírito Santo at the helm, losing 2-1 at Ewood Park to Blackburn Rovers.

With three Championship promotions on his CV, the former England midfielder knows exactly what it takes to guide a club out of this division, and is expected to be backed in January with automatic promotion in his sights.

Cesar Peixoto, meanwhile, will be intent on guiding Wolves back to the Premier League at the first opportunity after succeeding Rob Edwards at Molineux in the summer, and seems to be looking for that added spark up top in Naderi.

Whether either of them will be able to lure Naderi to England remains to be seen, though both of their aims have been made abundantly clear.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

It remains to be seen where Odogu will end up next, but in the summer, Anderlecht and Mainz also expressed an interest.

England have also progressed after their final Group L win over Panama, which keeps Reece James and Trevoh Chalobah inside the wider knockout picture.

For supporters, that is a status marker. For Alonso, it is where summer pride starts to become pre-season compression.

The concern is not simply volume. It is where the minutes are landing.

Enzo has not missed a minute for Argentina, while Caicedo has remained central for Ecuador and captained them in the 2-1 win over Germany.

Those are not fringe workloads. They are the two midfield profiles Chelsea have built large parts of their control game around.

Alonso’s first Chelsea pre-season was always going to demand clarity through the middle of the pitch.

Instead, the players most central to that reset are still operating in knockout football.

Caicedo’s Ecuador role is the cleanest example. Read Chelsea has already assessed how his captaincy display against Germany strengthened his leadership case, but the next layer is more complicated.

Every additional knockout tie gives him more tournament rhythm, more authority and more fatigue.

Argentina’s route against Cape Verde keeps him on a glamorous stage at exactly the moment Chelsea need to settle his role, mood and tactical responsibilities under Alonso.

If Madrid-related noise continues around him, the club cannot afford a sluggish July handover.

Reece James started England’s first two matches before a hamstring issue ruled him out of the Panama game, according to the Guardian.

England still topped the group and moved into a last-32 tie with DR Congo, but James’ situation is exactly the kind of detail Chelsea’s medical team will monitor closely.

Chelsea have already had to plan around a large World Cup group.

The broader issue was clear in Read Chelsea’s earlier pre-season workload piece, but the knockout stage sharpens it because the returns will now be staggered.

Enzo brings a maximum group-stage load. Caicedo brings captaincy responsibility and heavy Ecuador minutes.

James brings knockout qualification mixed with another hamstring-management question.

Gusto and Hato remain tied to France and the Netherlands, while Penders and Paez are still absorbing tournament environments before club reintegration.

That is a spine issue, not a fringe issue.

Alonso needs automatisms in possession, spacing around the double pivot, clearer full-back behaviour and pressing triggers from midfield.

Those details do not bed in properly when key players arrive in drips after a major summer tournament.

Players returning from knockout football can bring competitive sharpness that closed-door friendlies cannot replicate.

Caicedo captaining Ecuador through pressure games matters. Enzo playing high-leverage minutes for Argentina matters.

Gusto and Hato absorbing tournament detail also helps Chelsea’s defensive reset.

But Chelsea cannot treat every tournament minute as clean preparation.

Alonso must decide who returns straight into tactical work, who needs a genuine pause and who can handle August without creating an avoidable injury bill by September.

That is where the James situation feels central.

If Chelsea’s captain carries a World Cup hamstring warning, the club’s right-sided structure becomes more than a selection debate.

It becomes an availability question that touches Gusto, the wider defensive rebuild and the need for control from midfield.

Read Chelsea has already looked at James’ captaincy test under Alonso, and the injury layer now makes that test sharper.

This is the hidden cost of a strong international footprint.

Chelsea want players good enough to survive deep into tournaments.

Alonso now has to make sure that status does not leave his first league weeks short of the very control those players are meant to provide.

#2139. Michael Carrick handed lifeline ahead of Chelsea trip

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Michael Carrick has come under immense pressure as a disjointed Manchester United sit just two places above the relegation places.

Despite the scrutiny and the news of a possible sacking, Michael Carrick has been given another chance at Manchester United.

Michael Carrick will remain in charge of Manchester United for their Premier League trip to Chelsea on Saturday after the club decided to give their under-pressure head coach another opportunity to turn things around.

United have gone eight games without a win in all competitions, with Sunday’s humiliating 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth leaving them 16th in the Premier League table, just one point above the relegation zone.

Carrick’s position looked increasingly uncertain after the Bournemouth loss. As a result, United chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox were at Carrington on Monday to discuss the situation.

It has been reported by Sky Sports that United have considered other options internally as well to replace Micahel Carrick but for the weekend, they are sticking with Michael Carrick.

Carrick took training on Tuesday and the decision means he has at least one more match to convince the club’s hierarchy that he can get United back on track.

Gary Neville admitted he was surprised that Carrick had survived the immediate threat of losing his job, although he welcomed the decision.

“I am surprised, but I am happy because I was absolutely certain that what I witnessed on Sunday would be the end of Michael,” Neville said in Sky Sports.

The former United captain explained that Carrick’s actions after the Bournemouth defeat had made him think the manager was preparing to say goodbye to the supporters.

“It looked like he was walking round at the end on his own with the players behind him, like it was almost a goodbye lap,” Neville added.

Fans continued singing the manager’s name after the final whistle, while the rest of the team drew boos.

Despite his surprise, Gary Neville hopes Micahel Carrick can use the extra time to get a response from his squad.

“I hope he can somehow get those players to start playing for him again because they haven’t played for him in the last few weeks,” he said.

The trip to Chelsea now becomes a crucial test for Micahel Carrick but United need a result and a far better performance to ease the pressure at Old Trafford.

#2137. Serie A preview: Genoa vs. AC Milan – Team news, opposition insight, stats and prediction

A tough away trip awaits AC Milan on Sunday afternoon, hoping to restore some order against an in-form Genoa side.

What many hoped would be a positive week quickly turned into a negative one, as Milan failed to build on the derby win against Ferencvaros. In other words, they need to respond with a strong performance against Genoa, which is easier said than done.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Ruben Amorim didn’t have a press conference earlier today due to the tight schedule, so there are no new takeaways from him. Judging from the post-match interviews on Thursday, however, he’s expecting a lot more from his players at the Marassi.

Game date: Sunday, November 8 Kick-off time: 15:00 (CET) Venue: Stadio Luigi Ferraris Referee: Davide Massa (VAR: Camplone)

It’s been said for quite a few games now, but Amorim certainly isn’t being helped by the injury situation. In total, seven players will be on the sidelines for the game tomorrow, including key players like Samuel Chukwueze, Strahinja Pavlovic and Alexis Saelemaekers.

Milan were hoping to recover the Serbian for this one, but severe stomach pain is keeping him on the sidelines after missing the Ferencvaros game too. Considering that Matteo Gabbia and Fikayo Tomori were already on the injury list, it’s certainly not ideal.

Mike Maignan will be back between the sticks, at least, after serving a suspension in the Europa League. He will have a defence of Mario Gila, Koni De Winter, Sankhoun Diawara and Pervis Estupinan ahead of him, with the left flank being the main worry there.

In midfield, Ardon Jashari will be demoted to the bench as Luka Modric will be back from start, partnering with Yunus Musah in a tandem. Adrien Rabiot, meanwhile, is expected to have a slightly more advanced role.

Diego Moreira and Christian Pulisic will be the danger men on the attacking flanks, looking to replicate their success against Inter in the derby. The same cannot be said for Goncalo Ramos, who instead needs to get going with his goalscoring after bagging just two league goals so far.

In terms of firepower on the bench, Amorim could turn to Davide Bartesaghi, the aforementioned Jashari, Omari Hutchinson and Alphadjo Cisse. Francesco Camarda will also be there, of course, but whether he can be categorised as an impact player is a different question.

Predicted Milan XI (4-2-3-1): Maignan; Gila, De Winter, Diawara, Estupinan; Musah, Modric; Moreira, Rabiot, Pulisic; Ramos.

Predicted bench: Torriani, Bouyer, F. Terracciano, Vladimirov, Bartesaghi, Jashari, Comotto, Hutchinson, Cisse, Camarda.

Injuries & suspensions: Chukwueze, Gabbia, Loftus-Cheek, Pavlovic, Saelemaekers, Terracciano, Tomori.

After what was a disastrous start to the 2025-26 season under Patrick Vieira, sitting rock bottom between the 7th and 9th matchdays, Daniele De Rossi was appointed. He quickly brought them above the relegation zone and managed to keep them there, finishing seven points clear in the end.

It was only right to confirm him for this season, as such, and there have been no regrets so far. The start was admittedly dodgy, with one draw and four defeats in the first five games, but there have been four wins and one defeat in the five games since. Crisis very much averted.

The only defeat in the last five games came against De Rossi’s former club as well, namely Roma, who have been on fire at the start of this season. There can be no accusations of the former midfielder throwing the game to benefit his former club, let’s put it that way.

Nevertheless, the poor star has been cancelled out and it’s possible that De Rossoneri just needed some time, especially after losing key players like Jeff Ekhator (to Juventus) and Brooke Norton-Vuffy (to Hull City). The Serie A veteran Ruslan Malinovskyi also waved goodbye as his contract expired.

On the flip side, the Rossoblu did keep hold of most of the key players from last season, such as Johan Vasquez, Leo Ostigard, Morten Frendrup, and Lorenzo Colombo. The latter was signed permanently from Milan after a season-long loan, as the conditions for the obligation were triggered.

Some interesting signings were also made, from the veteran (and former Milan man) Stephan El Shaarawy to the familiar face Hamed Traore, who played for Sassuolo between 2019 and 2013. After spells at Bournemouth, Napoli, Auxerre and Marseille, he’s now looking to regain stability.

As for the latest team news, De Rossi has a much better situation than Amorim with no injuries or suspensions to note. Lorenzo Venturino and Elias Havel were both on the sidelines but have returned for this one.

It looks like the former Milan men El Shaarawy and Colombo will start, while Junior Messias could perhaps be introduced from the bench. De Rossi didn’t give much away during his presser in terms of the team selection, but he did make it clear that Genoa cannot get too cocky.

Genoa (3-5-2): Bijlow; Marcandalli, Ostigard, Vasquez; Ellertsson, Baldanzi, Frendrup, Traore, El Shaarawy; Colombo, Osmajic.

Predicted bench: Stolz, Sommariva, Messias, Otoa, Sabelli, Puczka, Amorim, Meichtry, Mitaj, Vitinha, Sow, Drameh, Ehizibue, Wiafe, Venturino, Havel.

This will be the 157th encounter between the two sides, with Milan looking to build on their 71 wins in the fixture, while Genoa are quite far behind at 37 wins. 48 games have ended all square, which is a tally that has increased lately.

In the last five games, there have been three draws (3-3, 0-0 and 1-1), but the good news for the Rossoneri is that they won the other two games. The five games before that were all wins as well, with no defeat recorded against the Rossoblu since 2020.

The most recent win was a 2-1 win at the Marassi in favour of Milan, courtesy of goals from Christopher Nkunku and Zachary Athekame. They both left the club on loan this summer, so they won’t be of any help tomorrow.

In the game before that, Rafael Leao (another player who’s no longer in the team) rescued a point in the dying minutes after Colombo had given Genoa the lead in the first half.

➤ Milan are unbeaten in their last 10 Serie A matches against Genoa (W6 D4). Since 2020-21, the Rossoneri only have a longer run of unbeaten games against Hellas Verona (12).

➤ Milan have won each of their last four Serie A away games against Genoa; only against three teams do the Rossoneri currently have a longer running away win streak in the competition: Hellas Verona (seven), Messina (five) and Pisa (five).

➤ Genoa are on a three-game winning streak in Serie A. The last time the Rossoblu recorded a streak of four consecutive wins in a single season in the top flight was between March and April 2009.

➤ Genoa have won three of their first six home games this season (D1 L2). The Rossoblu haven’t won four of their first seven Serie A home games since the 2015-16 season, when Gian Piero Gasperini was in charge.

➤ Ruben Amorim’s Milan have scored 21 goals in their first 10 Serie A games this season. The last Rossoneri manager to do better after his first 11 league games was Cesare Maldini in 1972, with 25 goals.

➤ Milan have won two and drawn three of their first five away games this season. The Rossoneri have avoided defeat in their first six away games of the season on three occasions between 2020-21 and 2025-26, the same number as Inter and Napoli, and could now become the only team to do so four times in this span, including the current season.

➤ Genoa have scored four goals in the first 15 minutes of Serie A action this season, a figure they share with Frosinone, Atalanta, Lazio, and Roma and second only to Milan (five). However, the Rossoblu are the only team yet to score in the final 15 minutes of a match.

➤ Stephan El Shaarawy, who scored 21 goals in 83 league appearances for AC Milan, has provided an assist in each of his last two Serie A appearances. Should he also contribute to a goal in his next match, he would become, at 34 years and 12 days, the second-oldest player to achieve this feat in three consecutive games for Genoa since 2004-05, after Domenico Criscito in 2021 (34 years and 269 days).

➤ Adrien Rabiot has bagged five goals and three assists in his first eight league appearances this season. The last midfielder to do better in the first nine games of a Serie A season was Sergej Milinkovic-Savic in 2022-23, with 10 contributions (three goals and seven assists).

Furthermore, the Frenchman has been involved in a goal in each of his last five appearances, and the last Milan midfielder to do better in Serie A was Kaka in 2004 (seven).

➤ Diego Moreira has been directly involved in seven goals (four goals and three assists) in his first six Serie A appearances for AC Milan. Since 2004/05, no player has had more in his first six league games for the Rossoneri, equaling Mario Balotelli’s record (seven in 2013).

Starting with Milan, and at the back, I think we should keep an extra eye on Diawara. He did well in the derby but after switching to centre-back for the Ferencvaros game, his display just wasn’t good enough. He will have the same position tomorrow and we need to see an improvement.

Further up the pitch, Moreira showed in the derby that he can be just the X-factor that Milan need, and this game should be an even better one for him (at least on paper). The cameo against Ferencvaros was positive despite the result, so hopefully he can build on the last week.

As for the home side, I’m curious to see Traore in action again. He definitely had some very good games for Sassuolo against the Rossoneri and with more consistent playing time at Genoa, he could rediscover that spark which made him so special.

Finally, I think it would be silly to ignore the defensive general, Vasquez. He always seems to have good games against Milan and honestly, he’s the type of player the Rossoneri could use right now, at least as a deputy.

It’s been incredibly hard to predict how well Milan will do so far this season, with many ups and downs. However, my gut feeling tells me that they will be able to do a whole lot better than on Thursday, especially since the key players are back from start.

My only worry is the left-hand side of the defence, with Diawara and Estupinan set to start there. As such, I’m going to go for a narrow win, with no clean sheet either. Let us know your prediction in the comments below!

#2136. Malo Gusto’s Kante Lesson Gives Chelsea A £75m Xabi Alonso Decision

Malo Gusto’s World Cup has suddenly become more than a France subplot for Chelsea. It is now a live assessment of whether Xabi Alonso inherits a right-back worth protecting, reshaping or trading at the peak of his market.

Chelsea’s own interview with Gusto this week underlined the human part of that equation. The 23-year-old spoke about learning from N’Golo Kante, calling the former Blue one of the best midfielders France have produced and stressing the value of his example on and off the pitch.

That matters because Kante is not just a nostalgic reference point. He remains the standard Chelsea supporters still use when judging humility, intensity and reliability in a high-pressure dressing room.

For Gusto, those qualities arrive at a decisive moment. Reports around the player’s future claim Chelsea value him at around £75million, with the imminent Marco Palestra deal creating a more crowded full-back picture.

That number looks aggressive, but the logic is clear. Gusto is young, capped by France, under contract, Premier League-tested and capable of playing in several defensive structures.

🚨🚨| NEW: Chelsea are open to selling Malo Gusto this summer, with his agent already in 𝐓𝐀𝐋𝐊𝐒 with Manchester City and Inter. Chelsea will only sell if their valuation is met.{@siphillipssport} pic.twitter.com/vNR2bWud9F

The easiest reading of Gusto’s Kante comments is sentimental. It should not be.

For Alonso, who officially begins work on July 1, the important detail is what those comments say about the player Chelsea have been developing.

Gusto has not always looked completely settled at Stamford Bridge. His best games have carried thrust, speed and recovery power, while his less convincing spells have raised questions about final-ball quality and defensive concentration.

That uncertainty is exactly why the next coach matters.

Alonso’s previous teams have leaned heavily on wide players who can receive under pressure, attack space early and still protect the back line when possession breaks down. Gusto has the athletic base for that job.

The test is whether his decision-making can be refined enough to make him a structural piece rather than a rotation asset.

That is where the Kante reference lands with force. Chelsea do not need Gusto to imitate Kante’s position. They need him to absorb the same professional standard: repeat actions, clean defensive choices, no wasted emotion and total commitment to the collective rhythm.

The Marco Palestra deal changes the right side of Chelsea’s squad immediately. Read Chelsea has already examined how Palestra’s arrival offers a tactical clue over Alonso’s defensive plans, and that pressure is now sharpened by market noise around Gusto.

Chelsea can frame the situation in two ways.

One is profit-led. If a club comes close to the reported £75million level, selling a player signed from Lyon for a much lower fee becomes a powerful PSR lever.

The other is football-led. If Alonso sees Gusto as a high-upside wing-back who fits his model, selling him just as the coaching environment becomes more suitable would carry long-term risk.

The complication is Reece James. The captain’s fitness issues, including his current England hamstring concern, mean Chelsea still need genuine right-sided depth.

Palestra may be part of the future, but Gusto is already part of the present. Chelsea have too often needed emergency solutions in that area to treat proven depth lightly.

That is why Read Chelsea’s recent look at James awaiting hamstring scan results matters in this debate. Right-back remains one of the most talented areas of the squad, but also one of the least straightforward.

This is why Gusto’s France rhythm is more valuable than a simple tournament diary entry. Chelsea are watching a player operate inside an elite international camp, learn from a club legend and handle a public transfer valuation that can change his status overnight.

Alonso’s first decision does not have to be final, but it does have to be clear.

If Gusto stays, he needs a defined role early in pre-season and a pathway that makes sense alongside Palestra and James. If Chelsea sell, they need to do it at a number that justifies weakening one of the few squad areas where youth, speed and top-level experience already overlap.

Gusto’s Kante lesson, then, is not just a feel-good Chelsea connection. It is a reminder of the standard Alonso must demand before deciding whether £75million is a price to accept or a warning sign that Chelsea should hold firm.

#2135. Two days or two weeks – Michael Carrick future decision rolls into the international break

Manchester United will use the November international break to decide whether Michael Carrick stays on as head coach, after a three-game reprieve that delivered only one win.

According to The Guardian, Carrick came close to being sacked after the 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth on 25 October.

Director of football Jason Wilcox and chief executive Omar Berrada discussed his position at the training ground the following day and agreed to give him three matches to turn things around before the break.

Those games brought a 2-0 defeat at Chelsea, a 3-2 win over Roma and a chaotic 4-4 draw with Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday. United conceded eight goals across the three fixtures, and defensive frailty remains the biggest worry.

Wilcox is now leading a review of Carrick’s position. He will consult Berrada before making a recommendation to Sir Jim Ratcliffe, who oversees the club’s sporting operations. Talks are expected to begin on Monday and continue through the break.

The league table makes grim reading. United sit in the Premier League relegation zone with just one league win in their last 10 matches.

If he does win a stay of execution, results will need to follow. Manchester United cannot be languishing in 17th place.

Carrick remains defiant. Speaking to Sky Sports News after the Villa draw, he admitted his side “haven’t won enough games” but insisted he expects to move forward.

“I’m quite clear with what direction the club needs to go in, and it’s got to win games, said Carrick after the late Villa draw.

“I think there’s more than enough [to turn things around].”

If United do make a change, former Newcastle manager Eddie Howe is reported to be Wilcox’s preferred candidate. Howe has been out of work since leaving St James’ Park during pre-season, after four and a half years in charge.

In the meantime, there are no plans to strengthen Carrick’s coaching staff.

For now, Carrick is expected to be in charge for United’s next match, against Liverpool. Whether three games were enough to convince the hierarchy should become clear before then.

The trip to Anfield could be do or die for the decorated former United midfielder.

He’s not likely to survive a heavy defeat at the home of the club’s fierce rivals, but a galvanising win would surely buy him time and goodwill.

That’s if he makes it through the next fortnight before then. A decision or indication could be two days or two weeks away.

The only certainty at Old Trafford is uncertainty.

#2134. Senegal Knockout Tie Gives Chelsea A Double Xabi Alonso Audit

Chelsea’s World Cup watch has moved beyond simple minutes management.

Senegal’s Round-of-32 meeting with Belgium now gives Xabi Alonso a live read on two very different development tracks.

Nicolas Jackson’s attacking authority is the immediate question. Mamadou Sarr’s defensive readiness is the longer-term one.

Chelsea confirmed that Senegal kept their knockout hopes alive with a 5-0 win over Iraq, with Jackson featuring from the bench.

Senegal have since moved into the knockouts, where they face Belgium in Seattle on Wednesday 1 July.

Jackson and Sarr travel into that game with contrasting club questions attached. Both still carry clear Chelsea relevance.

Sarr has not been the loudest Chelsea story of the tournament.

That may actually make this test more useful.

The 20-year-old is still moving through the awkward stage between prospect, squad option and saleable BlueCo asset.

Knockout football against Belgium can sharpen that picture quickly.

The timing is awkward in the best possible way for Chelsea. Alonso steps into a squad with expensive centre-backs, academy-trained options and a board still comfortable using the market aggressively.

A disciplined Sarr performance would not end that search. It would give the recruitment team a stronger internal benchmark before committing another major fee.

Chelsea need defenders who can hold a high line, cover wide channels and still pass through pressure. Belgium should ask those questions more directly than most group-stage opponents.

The context matters. Chelsea’s recall note recorded that Sarr returned from Strasbourg after 18 appearances across Ligue 1 and the Conference League.

The club also noted that he had already earned his first Senegal cap.

That is a different profile from an academy player being carried by reputation alone.

There is a statistical base behind the interest too. When Chelsea completed the deal, beIN SPORTS noted that Sarr made 27 Ligue 1 appearances in his first Strasbourg season.

He helped them keep 10 clean sheets. He also produced a team-high 20 blocks and made 25 interceptions.

Those numbers explain why this Belgium game is not just a sentimental World Cup subplot.

Alonso’s Chelsea defence is already crowded with Levi Colwill, Wesley Fofana, Trevoh Chalobah and external targets still orbiting the plan.

ReadChelsea has already looked at why Colwill’s recovery story gives Alonso a defensive test. Sarr’s value is different.

He is still being evaluated as a physical, long-range defensive project.

Jackson’s role changes the editorial weight of the tie.

Chelsea’s striker picture has been messy for long enough. Any high-pressure international performance now carries wider meaning.

ReadChelsea has already framed the forward’s tournament as a live Senegal striker audit for Chelsea.

Belgium should stress both Chelsea questions at once.

Jackson has to offer penalty-box threat and transition security. Sarr, if trusted, has to defend space against a technically cleaner opponent.

He also has to show whether his recovery pace and frame translate under tournament pressure.

Sarr’s own mentality profile is worth noting. In a Chelsea World Cup Q&A, he named Lionel Messi, Sadio Mane and Paul Pogba as his tournament heroes.

His answer on Pogba focused on leadership and group mentality.

That lands neatly with the exact question Chelsea now need answered. Is there enough presence to go with the physical tools?

This is why Belgium feels like a pathway check rather than a routine World Cup watch item.

Chelsea do not need Sarr to solve the defence in July. They need clearer evidence on whether he belongs in Alonso’s first pre-season group.

He could need another loan. He could also become part of the club’s centre-back market arithmetic.

For Jackson, the question is more immediate. For Sarr, it is more structural.

Together, Senegal’s knockout tie gives Chelsea a rare clean look at two players on opposite ends of the same squad-building argument.

Who can help now, and who is worth protecting for later?

#2132. Monaco summit adds unnecessary drama to Michael Carrick situation

Michael Carrick arrived at Carrington for Manchester United training as normal on Tuesday morning, while his bosses were preparing to decide his future in talks on the Mediterranean.

Chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox are flying to Monaco to meet Sir Jim Ratcliffe, with the BBC reporting that the pair will recommend Carrick is sacked.The Guardian has indicated representatives of the Glazer family will also be involved.

The club insists the Monaco summit was pre-planned and is not solely about the head coach and while that may be true, it hardly matters.By allowing the meeting to become public knowledge, United have turned a straightforward football decision into unnecessary drama, compounding an already difficult situation.

What does is say about Man United in 2026 that a manager is given three games to secure his job, then left to train the squad during an international break while the executives are flying off to rule on his future? It’s farcical.

The latest chapter is another muddled process.Wilcox and Berrada met at Carrington the day after the 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth on 25 October and agreed to give Carrick three more matches.A defeat at Chelsea, a win over Roma and a 4-4 draw with Aston Villa later, they appear to have reached the conclusion many expected a fortnight ago.

If the timeline holds, the decision will be made at Wednesday’s meeting, with an official announcement expected later in the week.United reportedly want to tell Carrick face to face, which means he could spend another day or two working under a cloud. In reality, it could be two days or two weeks.

The stakes could hardly be higher. United have won once in 10 league games, their worst-ever start to a Premier League season, and sit one point off the bottom in 18th place.

Should Carrick go, attention will turn quickly to his successor.ESPN reports that United States head coach Mauricio Pochettino is the favoured candidate, while Eddie Howe has been named as Wilcox’s preferred choice. Antonio Conte has also been mentioned.

Carrick deserves better than this.Whatever the outcome, a club that wants to be taken seriously should not be settling its manager’s future in the full glare of the media while he is still out on the training pitch.
